I don't feel connected to it at all. I feel like an alien sitting in their dining room. I feel like I don't really belong in that room with them anymore. Like they're living in this normal, happy world, and I'm just trapped in this cold, empty bubble, watching them from the outside. I try to nod and smile and pretend I'm engaged, but inside, I'm just completely detached from the whole thing. I don't feel the warmth, I don't feel the joy... I just feel this massive, echoing distance between me and the people I'm supposed to be closest to. It's like the accident didn't just break my ability to do my job, it broke my ability to just... be a person around other people. So, no, I don't go out with friends anymore. I can barely even sit through a dinner with my own family without feeling like a complete ghost.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself since the accident?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? No. No, doctor, I... I haven't had any thoughts like that. It's... it's actually almost the exact opposite, if that makes any sense. \n\nI mean, with everything that happened at the plant, the accident, the way everything just went so horribly wrong so fast... I'm terrified of getting hurt. I'm terrified of *anyone* getting hurt. The idea of intentionally doing something to myself? It just... it doesn't even compute in my brain right now. My brain is so hyper-focused on keeping everyone safe, on checking and double-checking every single little thing so disaster doesn't strike again, that the thought of causing harm on purpose is just... no. \n\nPlus, even with how disconnected I feel from my wife and my kids—like I was just saying, sitting at that dining room table feeling like a total ghost—I still know I have a responsibility to them. I'm still their father, you know? I'm still the provider. I still need to be here. If I did something like that... if I hurt myself, after all the trauma we've already been through with the retrofit disaster and the fallout from that... it would just completely destroy them. And I already feel guilty enough about what happened at work. I couldn't possibly add that to the pile. \n\nSo, no, even on the absolute darkest nights when I'm staring at the ceiling at 3:00 in the morning and my mind is just racing and replaying the machinery and the schedules over and over... I mean, I just want the noise to stop, I want the memories to stop, but I don't want to... I don't want to end it all, you know? I just want to figure out how to fix whatever is broken in my head so I can go back to being the guy I was before all this happened. I just want to be normal again, not... not hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks to the accident at the plant?\n\nPatient: Flashbacks... I mean, I don't know if they're exactly like what you see in the movies, where the whole room disappears, but... yes. God, yes. It happens constantly. And it's never when I expect it, either. It's the most mundane things that just... they just rip me right out of whatever I'm doing and drag me back to that day. \n\nFor example, just the other week, they were testing the standard plant alarms. Just a routine drill, right? We do them all the time, it's protocol. But the absolute second that siren went off, that high-pitched wail, my chest just completely seized up. Or even just sitting at my desk in my office—if I have to pull up a retrofit checklist, just a standard blank form that I've used a thousand times in my career to verify equipment specs... I look at those little empty boxes and my hands just start shaking. \n\nAnd the smells... doctor, the smells are honestly the worst part. If the HVAC kicks in a certain way and brings in a draft from the fabrication floor, and I catch even just a faint whiff of hot metal? It's over. I'm completely gone. It throws me right back into that exact second. It's not just a memory, it's like I'm physically standing there again. I can feel the heat in the air, and then... then I just see him. I see him lying there on the concrete floor. I can see the exact angle he was at, the way the machinery was just... \n\nAnd the noise. The yelling. I can hear every single person screaming for help, shouting to cut the main power, radioing frantically for the medics. It's so loud in my head, doctor. It's not just remembering that people yelled, it's *hearing* their voices all over again, cracking with panic. Every time I smell that hot metal or hear a buzzer, it's like my brain doesn't know that it's months later and I'm safe in my office. My brain thinks the accident is happening right then and there all over again. I'll just be gripping the edge of my desk, sweating through my shirt, hearing those screams echoing in my ears until someone knocks on my door and snaps me out of it. \n\nAnd then I have to sit there and pretend I'm fine? Pretend I didn't just watch a man's life get ruined right in front of my eyes for the hundredth time that week? It's exhausting. It's just completely exhausting, because I can't escape it. I can't turn off the plant alarms, I can't stop using checklists to do my job, and I work in a manufacturing plant, for God's sake, there's always going to be the smell of hot metal somewhere. So I'm just constantly a sitting duck, just sitting there waiting for the next normal, everyday thing to send me right back to seeing him on that floor.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities that remind you of the accident at the plant?\n\nPatient: Avoiding... yeah. Yeah, I do. I mean, as a manager, part of my job is being out there, right? I've always prided myself on being a hands-on guy, the kind of manager who doesn't just sit behind a desk looking at spreadsheets but actually gets out on the floor, talks to the operators, checks the equipment. But since it happened... I can't. I completely avoid that specific production line. I avoid it whenever I possibly can. \n\nIf I have to walk from the administrative wing over to the loading docks, I will literally map out a completely different route in my head before I even leave my office. I'll take the long way around, walk all the way through packaging, go down the back stairwell, whatever it takes, even if it adds ten or fifteen minutes to my walk, just so I don't have to pass by that section of the floor. And if there's an issue on that line that needs a manager's eyes on it? I just... I find an excuse. I'll call up one of the floor supervisors, or I'll grab my assistant manager, and I'll tell them I'm bogged down on a conference call or drowning in paperwork, and I'll just send someone else to walk it. I make them do it. I feel incredibly guilty about it, doctor, I really do, because I'm pushing my own responsibilities onto my team, but my feet literally will not move in that direction. The thought of standing in that specific spot again... I just can't do it.\n\nAnd it's not even just the physical places, it's... it's the talking, too. You know how it is in a manufacturing plant, people talk. It's a tight-knit group, and everyone has a theory or a rumor they heard. But the absolute second people start talking about the rollout, or if they bring up the ongoing investigation... I just shut it down. Immediately. If I walk into the breakroom to get a coffee and I hear two guys discussing what the safety inspectors are looking at, or what went wrong with the retrofit schedule, I will just cut them off right there. I interrupt them, I change the subject, or I just use my authority to tell them flat out that we are not discussing the investigation on company time. \n\nI know they probably talk about me behind my back. They probably think I'm being a hardass manager, or worse, they might think I'm trying to cover my own tracks by not letting anyone talk about it. But that's not it at all. It's just that if I let them keep talking, if I have to stand there and listen to the words 'investigation' or 'rollout' and hear them dissecting every little thing that happened that day... my chest gets so tight I feel like I'm going to pass out. I can't let my mind go back there. So I just shut the conversations down before they can even start. I have to control it, you know? Because if I don't avoid that line, and if I don't stop people from talking about it, I'm just going to fall completely apart right there in the middle of the plant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly alert or on the lookout for potential dangers?\n\nPatient: Overly alert... yeah. Yeah, you could definitely say that. It's like my brain is stuck in overdrive, just scanning, scanning, scanning, every single second of the day. I can't turn it off. \n\nEven though I try to avoid that one specific production line like I was just telling you, I still have to be out on the floor for other things, and when I am... it's exhausting. I am constantly, obsessively checking everything. I'll be walking down the aisle and I'm staring at the pressure gauges on the boilers, reading the dials, making sure they're in the green, even though we have dedicated operators for that. I'm constantly checking where the nearest exits are, calculating in my head exactly how many seconds it would take to get a crew out of there if something blew. \n\nAnd the guys working the machines... I find myself just staring at their hands. Just watching their hands like a hawk, making sure they aren't getting too close to the pinch points, making sure they aren't rushing and skipping a safety step. I'm constantly mapping out exactly where every single emergency stop button is. Like, if I'm standing near assembly station four, my brain is repeating, 'The E-stop is exactly three paces to your left, just reach out your left hand.' I rehearse it over and over. I have to know exactly how to shut everything down in a split second.\n\nAnd it doesn't even stop when I get off the floor. That's the crazy part. Even when I'm in the administrative wing, where it's quiet and perfectly safe... like, when we have our weekly management meetings in the main conference room. I used to just stroll in right at 9:00 AM, coffee in hand, and grab whatever chair was empty. I can't do that anymore. I literally have to show up five or ten minutes early now just so I can pick a very specific seat. I have to sit at the far end of the table where I can see the door. I have to have a clear line of sight to the hallway. \n\nIf I get there late and the only open chair is the one with its back to the door? Forget it. I won't hear a single word of the quarterly reports or the budget projections. The whole time, my heart will just be pounding, and I'm just sitting there waiting for the door to burst open, waiting for someone to run in screaming that there's been another accident. I just... I have to see everything coming. I have to be ready. It's like I'm just waiting for the other shoe to drop, constantly, and if I let my guard down for even one second, someone else is going to get hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities since the accident?\n\nPatient: Focusing? I mean... no, I wouldn't say I have trouble focusing on my actual tasks. It's kind of funny you ask that, actually, because my assistant manager—who sort of had to step up and cover for me a bit right after it happened—he thought I'd be dropping the ball on the administrative side of things. But honestly, when I sit down at my desk, I can still grind through a fifty-page quarterly projection report without missing a beat. \n\nI mean, sure, like I was telling you earlier, if a siren goes off or if I smell that hot metal, then yeah, I'm completely derailed. But if it's just me, in my office, with the door closed and my back to the wall, and I know I'm safe? I can focus just fine. I still read through all the supply chain logistics, I still check all the employee timesheets... I've always been a really detail-oriented guy. You don't get to be a manager at a manufacturing plant without being able to just lock in on a task and see it through. \n\nSo the focus itself really isn't the problem. I can still sit there and draft up the weekly memos, or review the routine maintenance logs—well, the ones that aren't from that specific line, obviously. I just lock myself in my office and I do the work. Sometimes, honestly, the paperwork is the only thing I *can* focus on. Just staring at the numbers on a spreadsheet, line by line, column by column, just pouring all my attention into that so I don't have to think about... well, anything else. It's almost like a distraction, you know? Just me and the data. So, no, I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration when it comes to actually getting my day-to-day desk work done. If anything, I might be burying myself in those tasks a little too much just to keep my mind occupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No... no, I can't say I've ever experienced anything like that. Honestly, doctor, it's almost the exact opposite for me. I feel like I'm way too trapped inside my body, if that makes sense. \n\nI mean, I've heard of people having that kind of thing, you know, where they say they feel like they're floating above themselves or like they're watching a movie of their own life playing out. But for me? I am painfully, constantly aware of every single thing my physical body is doing. When I'm walking the floor, or even just sitting in that conference room like I was telling you about earlier, I feel every single beat of my heart slamming against my ribs. I can feel the sweat starting on my palms, I can feel exactly how tight my shoulders are... I'm constantly clenching my jaw, actually, to the point where my teeth literally ache by the time I get in my truck to drive home at the end of the shift. \n\nSo, no, I'm definitely not floating away or observing myself from the ceiling or anything like that. I'm just... I'm locked in. I am completely, one-hundred percent stuck right here in my own skin, just feeling every little jolt of adrenaline and every muscle tensing up. I mean, sometimes I think maybe it would actually be nice to just check out for a minute and float away, you know? Just to get a five-minute break from the constant physical tension of it all. But no, that doesn't happen to me. I'm very much grounded in my physical self, probably more than I ever was before the accident, just because I'm so hyper-aware of every physical sensation now. So, no, definitely no out-of-body stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

If anything, I might be burying myself in those tasks a little too much just to keep my mind occupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No... no, I can't say I've ever experienced anything like that. Honestly, doctor, it's almost the exact opposite for me. I feel like I'm way too trapped inside my body, if that makes sense. \n\nI mean, I've heard of people having that kind of thing, you know, where they say they feel like they're floating above themselves or like they're watching a movie of their own life playing out. But for me? I am painfully, constantly aware of every single thing my physical body is doing. When I'm walking the floor, or even just sitting in that conference room like I was telling you about earlier, I feel every single beat of my heart slamming against my ribs. I can feel the sweat starting on my palms, I can feel exactly how tight my shoulders are... I'm constantly clenching my jaw, actually, to the point where my teeth literally ache by the time I get in my truck to drive home at the end of the shift. \n\nSo, no, I'm definitely not floating away or observing myself from the ceiling or anything like that. I'm just... I'm locked in. I am completely, one-hundred percent stuck right here in my own skin, just feeling every little jolt of adrenaline and every muscle tensing up. I mean, sometimes I think maybe it would actually be nice to just check out for a minute and float away, you know? Just to get a five-minute break from the constant physical tension of it all. But no, that doesn't happen to me. I'm very much grounded in my physical self, probably more than I ever was before the accident, just because I'm so hyper-aware of every physical sensation now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Disconnected from my emotions? No... no, definitely not. Honestly, just like with the physical stuff I was just telling you about, it’s the exact opposite. I feel like the volume dial on every single emotion I have is just cranked all the way up to maximum, twenty-four hours a day. 

I don't feel numb at all. If anything, I feel everything way too intensely now. Like, the fear and the stress... it’s not some distant, foggy thing. It is right there, sitting heavy right on my chest all the time. And it’s not just the fear, it’s everything else, too. Like just the other night, my wife asked me a totally normal, simple question about what we were doing for dinner, and I just snapped. I felt this massive, overwhelming surge of frustration and anger just bubble up out of nowhere. I didn't want to be mad, but the emotion was just so raw and so close to the surface. 

I remember hearing once that when people go through terrible things, sometimes they just shut down. Their minds just sort of go blank to protect them. And honestly, I kept waiting for that to happen to me. I kept thinking that any day now, I would just go numb and I wouldn't have to feel this constant, heavy dread anymore. I figured my brain would eventually just decide it had had enough and pull the plug, just to give me a little emotional vacation from the stress of it all. 

But it never happened. Every single morning I wake up and that dread is right there, fresh as ever. I feel the stress, I feel the edge, I feel every single bit of it. It’s exhausting, carrying all that emotion around like a hundred-pound backpack. Sometimes I look at the other guys at the plant, the ones who weren't on shift that day, and they’re just joking around, drinking their coffee, totally relaxed, and I can't even fathom feeling that light anymore. I am just so weighed down by feeling absolutely everything. So, no, I definitely don't feel disconnected from my emotions. I am way, way too plugged in.

The thought of standing in that specific spot again... I just can't do it.\n\nAnd it's not even just the physical places, it's... it's the talking, too. I interrupt them, I change the subject, or I just use my authority to tell them flat out that we are not discussing the investigation on company time. \n\nI know they probably talk about me behind my back. They probably think I'm being a hardass manager, or worse, they might think I'm trying to cover my own tracks by not letting anyone talk about it. But that's not it at all. It's just that if I let them keep talking, if I have to stand there and listen to the words 'investigation' or 'rollout' and hear them dissecting every little thing that happened that day... my chest gets so tight I feel like I'm going to pass out. I can't let my mind go back there. So I just shut the conversations down before they can even start. I have to control it, you know? Because if I don't avoid that line, and if I don't stop people from talking about it, I'm just going to fall completely apart right there in the middle of the plant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly alert or on the lookout for potential dangers?\n\nPatient: Overly alert... yeah. Yeah, you could definitely say that. It's like my brain is stuck in overdrive, just scanning, scanning, scanning, every single second of the day. I can't turn it off. \n\nEven though I try to avoid that one specific production line like I was just telling you, I still have to be out on the floor for other things, and when I am... it's exhausting. I am constantly, obsessively checking everything. I'll be walking down the aisle and I'm staring at the pressure gauges on the boilers, reading the dials, making sure they're in the green, even though we have dedicated operators for that. I'm constantly checking where the nearest exits are, calculating in my head exactly how many seconds it would take to get a crew out of there if something blew. \n\nAnd the guys working the machines... I find myself just staring at their hands. I literally have to show up five or ten minutes early now just so I can pick a very specific seat. I have to sit at the far end of the table where I can see the door. I have to have a clear line of sight to the hallway. \n\nIf I get there late and the only open chair is the one with its back to the door? Forget it. I won't hear a single word of the quarterly reports or the budget projections. The whole time, my heart will just be pounding, and I'm just sitting there waiting for the door to burst open, waiting for someone to run in screaming that there's been another accident. I just... I have to see everything coming. I have to be ready. It's like I'm just waiting for the other shoe to drop, constantly, and if I let my guard down for even one second, someone else is going to get hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities since the accident?\n\nPatient: Focusing? I mean... no, I wouldn't say I have trouble focusing on my actual tasks. But honestly, when I sit down at my desk, I can still grind through a fifty-page quarterly projection report without missing a beat. \n\nI mean, sure, like I was telling you earlier, if a siren goes off or if I smell that hot metal, then yeah, I'm completely derailed. But if it's just me, in my office, with the door closed and my back to the wall, and I know I'm safe? I can focus just fine. I still read through all the supply chain logistics, I still check all the employee timesheets... I've always been a really detail-oriented guy. You don't get to be a manager at a manufacturing plant without being able to just lock in on a task and see it through. \n\nSo the focus itself really isn't the problem. I can still sit there and draft up the weekly memos, or review the routine maintenance logs—well, the ones that aren't from that specific line, obviously. I just lock myself in my office and I do the work. Sometimes, honestly, the paperwork is the only thing I *can* focus on. Just staring at the numbers on a spreadsheet, line by line, column by column, just pouring all my attention into that so I don't have to think about... well, anything else. It's almost like a distraction, you know? Just me and the data. So, no, I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration when it comes to actually getting my day-to-day desk work done. If anything, I might be burying myself in those tasks a little too much just to keep my mind occupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No... no, I can't say I've ever experienced anything like that. Honestly, doctor, it's almost the exact opposite for me. I feel like I'm way too trapped inside my body, if that makes sense. \n\nI mean, I've heard of people having that kind of thing, you know, where they say they feel like they're floating above themselves or like they're watching a movie of their own life playing out. But for me? I am painfully, constantly aware of every single thing my physical body is doing. When I'm walking the floor, or even just sitting in that conference room like I was telling you about earlier, I feel every single beat of my heart slamming against my ribs. I can feel the sweat starting on my palms, I can feel exactly how tight my shoulders are... I'm constantly clenching my jaw, actually, to the point where my teeth literally ache by the time I get in my truck to drive home at the end of the shift. \n\nSo, no, I'm definitely not floating away or observing myself from the ceiling or anything like that. I'm just... I'm locked in. I am completely, one-hundred percent stuck right here in my own skin, just feeling every little jolt of adrenaline and every muscle tensing up. I mean, sometimes I think maybe it would actually be nice to just check out for a minute and float away, you know? Just to get a five-minute break from the constant physical tension of it all. But no, that doesn't happen to me. I'm very much grounded in my physical self, probably more than I ever was before the accident, just because I'm so hyper-aware of every physical sensation now. So, no, definitely no out-of-body stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disconnected from yourself or your emotions at times?\n\nPatient: Disconnected from my emotions? No... no, definitely not. Honestly, just like with the physical stuff I was just telling you about, it’s the exact opposite. I feel like the volume dial on every single emotion I have is just cranked all the way up to maximum, twenty-four hours a day. \n\nI don't feel numb at all. If anything, I feel everything way too intensely now. Like, the fear and the stress... it’s not some distant, foggy thing. It is right there, sitting heavy right on my chest all the time. And it’s not just the fear, it’s everything else, too. Like just the other night, my wife asked me a totally normal, simple question about what we were doing for dinner, and I just snapped. I felt this massive, overwhelming surge of frustration and anger just bubble up out of nowhere. I didn't want to be mad, but the emotion was just so raw and so close to the surface. \n\nI remember hearing once that when people go through terrible things, sometimes they just shut down. Their minds just sort of go blank to protect them. And honestly, I kept waiting for that to happen to me. I kept thinking that any day now, I would just go numb and I wouldn't have to feel this constant, heavy dread anymore. I figured my brain would eventually just decide it had had enough and pull the plug, just to give me a little emotional vacation from the stress of it all. \n\nBut it never happened. Every single morning I wake up and that dread is right there, fresh as ever. I feel the stress, I feel the edge, I feel every single bit of it. It’s exhausting, carrying all that emotion around like a hundred-pound backpack. Sometimes I look at the other guys at the plant, the ones who weren't on shift that day, and they’re just joking around, drinking their coffee, totally relaxed, and I can't even fathom feeling that light anymore. I am just so weighed down by feeling absolutely everything. So, no, I definitely don't feel disconnected from my emotions. I am way, way too plugged in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Startled? Oh, God, yes. Yes, all the time. It's... it's honestly one of the worst parts of my day now. It's like my nervous system is just completely fried, just constantly set to a hair-trigger. 

I mean, working in a manufacturing plant, it's not a quiet environment, you know? There's always something going on. But before the accident, it was just background noise. Now? If a forklift backs up and that little reverse beeper goes off, or if someone accidentally bangs a heavy metal door shut down the hall, or even if one of the routine plant alarms just chirps for a second—which happens all the time for completely harmless reasons, like a pressure valve resetting—my whole body just reacts before my brain even knows what's happening. 

I physically jump. Like, a massive jolt goes right through me, like I literally think something is exploding all over again. My shoulders fly up to my ears, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears, and I just freeze. And the worst part is, it doesn't just pass in a second. You'd think, okay, you realize it's just a door, you calm down. But I don't. The adrenaline just floods my system, and it takes me several minutes—sometimes five or ten minutes—just sitting there, gripping the edge of my desk or leaning against a wall, before my hands actually stop shaking enough for me to even hold a pen or type on my keyboard. 

And it's so embarrassing, too. I'm the manager, right? I'm supposed to be the steady one. But the other day, my assistant manager—the one I mentioned earlier—he just dropped a clipboard on the concrete floor near the breakroom. Just a plastic clipboard. It made this sharp *smack* sound, and I practically jumped out of my skin. I spilled my coffee all over my shirt, and I was just standing there, panting like I'd run a marathon, while he's looking at me like I'm completely losing my mind. I had to go lock myself in my office and just put my head between my knees until the shaking stopped. So, yeah, small noises, surprises... they absolutely wreck me now. It's like I have no filter for it anymore. Every sudden sound is a threat.

If I walk into the breakroom to get a coffee and I hear two guys discussing what the safety inspectors are looking at, or what went wrong with the retrofit schedule, I will just cut them off right there. I interrupt them, I change the subject, or I just use my authority to tell them flat out that we are not discussing the investigation on company time. \n\nI know they probably talk about me behind my back. They probably think I'm being a hardass manager, or worse, they might think I'm trying to cover my own tracks by not letting anyone talk about it. But that's not it at all. It's just that if I let them keep talking, if I have to stand there and listen to the words 'investigation' or 'rollout' and hear them dissecting every little thing that happened that day... my chest gets so tight I feel like I'm going to pass out. I can't let my mind go back there. So I just shut the conversations down before they can even start. I have to control it, you know? Because if I don't avoid that line, and if I don't stop people from talking about it, I'm just going to fall completely apart right there in the middle of the plant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly alert or on the lookout for potential dangers?\n\nPatient: Overly alert... yeah. Yeah, you could definitely say that. It's like my brain is stuck in overdrive, just scanning, scanning, scanning, every single second of the day. I can't turn it off. \n\nEven though I try to avoid that one specific production line like I was just telling you, I still have to be out on the floor for other things, and when I am... it's exhausting. I am constantly, obsessively checking everything. I'll be walking down the aisle and I'm staring at the pressure gauges on the boilers, reading the dials, making sure they're in the green, even though we have dedicated operators for that. I'm constantly checking where the nearest exits are, calculating in my head exactly how many seconds it would take to get a crew out of there if something blew. \n\nAnd the guys working the machines... I find myself just staring at their hands. Just watching their hands like a hawk, making sure they aren't getting too close to the pinch points, making sure they aren't rushing and skipping a safety step. I'm constantly mapping out exactly where every single emergency stop button is. Like, if I'm standing near assembly station four, my brain is repeating, 'The E-stop is exactly three paces to your left, just reach out your left hand.' I rehearse it over and over. I have to know exactly how to shut everything down in a split second.\n\nAnd it doesn't even stop when I get off the floor. That's the crazy part. Even when I'm in the administrative wing, where it's quiet and perfectly safe... like, when we have our weekly management meetings in the main conference room. I used to just stroll in right at 9:00 AM, coffee in hand, and grab whatever chair was empty. I can't do that anymore. I literally have to show up five or ten minutes early now just so I can pick a very specific seat. I have to sit at the far end of the table where I can see the door. I have to have a clear line of sight to the hallway. \n\nIf I get there late and the only open chair is the one with its back to the door? Forget it. I won't hear a single word of the quarterly reports or the budget projections. The whole time, my heart will just be pounding, and I'm just sitting there waiting for the door to burst open, waiting for someone to run in screaming that there's been another accident. I just... I have to see everything coming. I have to be ready. It's like I'm just waiting for the other shoe to drop, constantly, and if I let my guard down for even one second, someone else is going to get hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities since the accident?\n\nPatient: Focusing? I mean... no, I wouldn't say I have trouble focusing on my actual tasks. But honestly, when I sit down at my desk, I can still grind through a fifty-page quarterly projection report without missing a beat. \n\nI mean, sure, like I was telling you earlier, if a siren goes off or if I smell that hot metal, then yeah, I'm completely derailed. I can still sit there and draft up the weekly memos, or review the routine maintenance logs—well, the ones that aren't from that specific line, obviously. I just lock myself in my office and I do the work. Sometimes, honestly, the paperwork is the only thing I *can* focus on. Just staring at the numbers on a spreadsheet, line by line, column by column, just pouring all my attention into that so I don't have to think about... well, anything else. It's almost like a distraction, you know? Just me and the data. So, no, I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration when it comes to actually getting my day-to-day desk work done. If anything, I might be burying myself in those tasks a little too much just to keep my mind occupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No... no, I can't say I've ever experienced anything like that. Honestly, doctor, it's almost the exact opposite for me. I feel like I'm way too trapped inside my body, if that makes sense. \n\nI mean, I've heard of people having that kind of thing, you know, where they say they feel like they're floating above themselves or like they're watching a movie of their own life playing out. But for me? I am painfully, constantly aware of every single thing my physical body is doing. When I'm walking the floor, or even just sitting in that conference room like I was telling you about earlier, I feel every single beat of my heart slamming against my ribs. I can feel the sweat starting on my palms, I can feel exactly how tight my shoulders are... I'm constantly clenching my jaw, actually, to the point where my teeth literally ache by the time I get in my truck to drive home at the end of the shift. \n\nSo, no, I'm definitely not floating away or observing myself from the ceiling or anything like that. I'm just... I'm locked in. I am completely, one-hundred percent stuck right here in my own skin, just feeling every little jolt of adrenaline and every muscle tensing up. I mean, sometimes I think maybe it would actually be nice to just check out for a minute and float away, you know? Just to get a five-minute break from the constant physical tension of it all. But no, that doesn't happen to me. I'm very much grounded in my physical self, probably more than I ever was before the accident, just because I'm so hyper-aware of every physical sensation now. So, no, definitely no out-of-body stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disconnected from yourself or your emotions at times?\n\nPatient: Disconnected from my emotions? No... no, definitely not. Honestly, just like with the physical stuff I was just telling you about, it’s the exact opposite. I felt this massive, overwhelming surge of frustration and anger just bubble up out of nowhere. I didn't want to be mad, but the emotion was just so raw and so close to the surface. \n\nI remember hearing once that when people go through terrible things, sometimes they just shut down. Their minds just sort of go blank to protect them. And honestly, I kept waiting for that to happen to me. I kept thinking that any day now, I would just go numb and I wouldn't have to feel this constant, heavy dread anymore. I am just so weighed down by feeling absolutely everything. So, no, I definitely don't feel disconnected from my emotions. I am way, way too plugged in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself startled by small noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Startled? Oh, God, yes. Yes, all the time. There's always something going on. But before the accident, it was just background noise. Now? If a forklift backs up and that little reverse beeper goes off, or if someone accidentally bangs a heavy metal door shut down the hall, or even if one of the routine plant alarms just chirps for a second—which happens all the time for completely harmless reasons, like a pressure valve resetting—my whole body just reacts before my brain even knows what's happening. \n\nI physically jump. Like, a massive jolt goes right through me, like I literally think something is exploding all over again. My shoulders fly up to my ears, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears, and I just freeze. And the worst part is, it doesn't just pass in a second. You'd think, okay, you realize it's just a door, you calm down. But I don't. The adrenaline just floods my system, and it takes me several minutes—sometimes five or ten minutes—just sitting there, gripping the edge of my desk or leaning against a wall, before my hands actually stop shaking enough for me to even hold a pen or type on my keyboard. \n\nAnd it's so embarrassing, too. I'm the manager, right? I'm supposed to be the steady one. But the other day, my assistant manager—the one I mentioned earlier—he just dropped a clipboard on the concrete floor near the breakroom. Just a plastic clipboard. It made this sharp *smack* sound, and I practically jumped out of my skin. I spilled my coffee all over my shirt, and I was just standing there, panting like I'd run a marathon, while he's looking at me like I'm completely losing my mind. I had to go lock myself in my office and just put my head between my knees until the shaking stopped. So, yeah, small noises, surprises... they absolutely wreck me now. It's like I have no filter for it anymore. Every sudden sound is a threat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I still read through all the supply chain logistics, I still check all the employee timesheets... I've always been a really detail-oriented guy. You don't get to be a manager at a manufacturing plant without being able to just lock in on a task and see it through. \n\nSo the focus itself really isn't the problem. I can still sit there and draft up the weekly memos, or review the routine maintenance logs—well, the ones that aren't from that specific line, obviously. I just lock myself in my office and I do the work. Sometimes, honestly, the paperwork is the only thing I *can* focus on. Just staring at the numbers on a spreadsheet, line by line, column by column, just pouring all my attention into that so I don't have to think about... well, anything else. It's almost like a distraction, you know? Just me and the data. So, no, I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration when it comes to actually getting my day-to-day desk work done. If anything, I might be burying myself in those tasks a little too much just to keep my mind occupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No... no, I can't say I've ever experienced anything like that. Honestly, doctor, it's almost the exact opposite for me. I feel like I'm way too trapped inside my body, if that makes sense. \n\nI mean, I've heard of people having that kind of thing, you know, where they say they feel like they're floating above themselves or like they're watching a movie of their own life playing out. But for me? I am painfully, constantly aware of every single thing my physical body is doing. When I'm walking the floor, or even just sitting in that conference room like I was telling you about earlier, I feel every single beat of my heart slamming against my ribs. I can feel the sweat starting on my palms, I can feel exactly how tight my shoulders are... I'm constantly clenching my jaw, actually, to the point where my teeth literally ache by the time I get in my truck to drive home at the end of the shift. \n\nSo, no, I'm definitely not floating away or observing myself from the ceiling or anything like that. I'm just... I'm locked in. I am completely, one-hundred percent stuck right here in my own skin, just feeling every little jolt of adrenaline and every muscle tensing up. I mean, sometimes I think maybe it would actually be nice to just check out for a minute and float away, you know? Just to get a five-minute break from the constant physical tension of it all. But no, that doesn't happen to me. I'm very much grounded in my physical self, probably more than I ever was before the accident, just because I'm so hyper-aware of every physical sensation now. So, no, definitely no out-of-body stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disconnected from yourself or your emotions at times?\n\nPatient: Disconnected from my emotions? No... no, definitely not. Honestly, just like with the physical stuff I was just telling you about, it’s the exact opposite. I feel like the volume dial on every single emotion I have is just cranked all the way up to maximum, twenty-four hours a day. \n\nI don't feel numb at all. If anything, I feel everything way too intensely now. Like, the fear and the stress... it’s not some distant, foggy thing. It is right there, sitting heavy right on my chest all the time. And it’s not just the fear, it’s everything else, too. Like just the other night, my wife asked me a totally normal, simple question about what we were doing for dinner, and I just snapped. I felt this massive, overwhelming surge of frustration and anger just bubble up out of nowhere. I didn't want to be mad, but the emotion was just so raw and so close to the surface. \n\nI remember hearing once that when people go through terrible things, sometimes they just shut down. Their minds just sort of go blank to protect them. And honestly, I kept waiting for that to happen to me. I kept thinking that any day now, I would just go numb and I wouldn't have to feel this constant, heavy dread anymore. I figured my brain would eventually just decide it had had enough and pull the plug, just to give me a little emotional vacation from the stress of it all. \n\nBut it never happened. Every single morning I wake up and that dread is right there, fresh as ever. I feel the stress, I feel the edge, I feel every single bit of it. It’s exhausting, carrying all that emotion around like a hundred-pound backpack. Sometimes I look at the other guys at the plant, the ones who weren't on shift that day, and they’re just joking around, drinking their coffee, totally relaxed, and I can't even fathom feeling that light anymore. I am just so weighed down by feeling absolutely everything. So, no, I definitely don't feel disconnected from my emotions. I am way, way too plugged in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself startled by small noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Startled? Oh, God, yes. Yes, all the time. It's... it's honestly one of the worst parts of my day now. It's like my nervous system is just completely fried, just constantly set to a hair-trigger. \n\nI mean, working in a manufacturing plant, it's not a quiet environment, you know? There's always something going on. But before the accident, it was just background noise. Now? If a forklift backs up and that little reverse beeper goes off, or if someone accidentally bangs a heavy metal door shut down the hall, or even if one of the routine plant alarms just chirps for a second—which happens all the time for completely harmless reasons, like a pressure valve resetting—my whole body just reacts before my brain even knows what's happening. \n\nI physically jump. Like, a massive jolt goes right through me, like I literally think something is exploding all over again. My shoulders fly up to my ears, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears, and I just freeze. And the worst part is, it doesn't just pass in a second. You'd think, okay, you realize it's just a door, you calm down. But I don't. The adrenaline just floods my system, and it takes me several minutes—sometimes five or ten minutes—just sitting there, gripping the edge of my desk or leaning against a wall, before my hands actually stop shaking enough for me to even hold a pen or type on my keyboard. \n\nAnd it's so embarrassing, too. I'm the manager, right? I'm supposed to be the steady one. But the other day, my assistant manager—the one I mentioned earlier—he just dropped a clipboard on the concrete floor near the breakroom. Just a plastic clipboard. It made this sharp *smack* sound, and I practically jumped out of my skin. I spilled my coffee all over my shirt, and I was just standing there, panting like I'd run a marathon, while he's looking at me like I'm completely losing my mind. I had to go lock myself in my office and just put my head between my knees until the shaking stopped. So, yeah, small noises, surprises... they absolutely wreck me now. It's like I have no filter for it anymore. Every sudden sound is a threat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. It never stops. It’s like my brain is just constantly scanning, scanning, scanning, looking for the next disaster, no matter where I am. 

You asked about not being at work, and yeah, it definitely happens at the grocery store or a restaurant—I'm always looking for the exits and figuring out my escape route—but honestly, it’s at the plant where it’s just completely out of control. It's exhausting. Before... well, before, I used to just walk the floor, you know? I'd talk to the guys, check the production numbers, just do standard manager stuff. But now? Now it's like I'm some kind of safety inspector on steroids. 

When I'm out on the floor, I am constantly—and I mean *constantly*—checking the gauges. Every single pressure gauge I pass, my eyes are just glued to it, making sure the needle is right where it's supposed to be. And the exits... I'm always looking at the exits. I'm mentally calculating exactly how many steps it would take to get to the nearest door from wherever I'm standing. I'm even watching people's hands. I know that probably sounds a little crazy, but I can't help it. I'm watching what my guys are doing with their hands, what tools they're holding, watching to see if they're about to make a mistake or drop something or turn a valve the wrong way. I make sure I know exactly where the emergency stops are on every single piece of equipment I walk past. I'll just be walking down the aisle, and my brain is just ticking them off: *E-stop is there, E-stop is there, E-stop is there.* 

And it's not even just when I'm out in the noisy parts of the plant. Even when we're just in the office area, doing administrative stuff... like, if we have a production meeting in the conference room? I literally cannot focus on the meeting if I don't get the right chair. I have to pick a seat where I can see the door. If I walk in late and the only empty chair has its back to the hallway, I will literally make up an excuse to stand against the back wall, or I'll ask someone to switch with me. I just can't have my back exposed like that. I have to be able to see who or what is coming through that door at all times. 

So, yeah, I am always on the lookout. Always. I can never just relax and exist in a room anymore. I have to know all the variables. I have to know the escape routes. I just... I can't ever turn it off, doctor. It's like I'm just waiting for the next shoe to drop, twenty-four hours a day.

Like, a massive jolt goes right through me, like I literally think something is exploding all over again. My shoulders fly up to my ears, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears, and I just freeze. And the worst part is, it doesn't just pass in a second. You'd think, okay, you realize it's just a door, you calm down. But I don't. Just a plastic clipboard. It made this sharp *smack* sound, and I practically jumped out of my skin. I spilled my coffee all over my shirt, and I was just standing there, panting like I'd run a marathon, while he's looking at me like I'm completely losing my mind. I had to go lock myself in my office and just put my head between my knees until the shaking stopped. So, yeah, small noises, surprises... they absolutely wreck me now. It's like I have no filter for it anymore. It’s like my brain is just constantly scanning, scanning, scanning, looking for the next disaster, no matter where I am. \n\nYou asked about not being at work, and yeah, it definitely happens at the grocery store or a restaurant—I'm always looking for the exits and figuring out my escape route—but honestly, it’s at the plant where it’s just completely out of control. Every single pressure gauge I pass, my eyes are just glued to it, making sure the needle is right where it's supposed to be. And the exits... I'm always looking at the exits. I'm mentally calculating exactly how many steps it would take to get to the nearest door from wherever I'm standing. I'm even watching people's hands. I know that probably sounds a little crazy, but I can't help it. I'm watching what my guys are doing with their hands, what tools they're holding, watching to see if they're about to make a mistake or drop something or turn a valve the wrong way. I'll just be walking down the aisle, and my brain is just ticking them off: *E-stop is there, E-stop is there, E-stop is there.* \n\nAnd it's not even just when I'm out in the noisy parts of the plant. I just can't have my back exposed like that. I have to be able to see who or what is coming through that door at all times. \n\nSo, yeah, I am always on the lookout. Always. I can never just relax and exist in a room anymore. I have to know all the variables. I have to know the escape routes. I just... I can't ever turn it off, doctor. I'm just going through the motions every single day, at work and at home, and I can't seem to find a way to actually feel good about anything anymore. It's just... flat. Everything is just completely flat, and I don't know how to get those feelings back.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I know the exact timeframe, unfortunately. It’s... let me think, it’s been about a year and seven months. Exactly a year and seven months, actually. It all started right after the retrofit accident at work. \n\nI remember the timeline so clearly because we were in the middle of this massive overhaul project—since I'm the manager, I had been coordinating the logistics for that retrofit for the better part of a year before it even started. We had so many moving parts, and everything was scheduled down to the absolute minute, you know? And then... well, the accident happened. And literally ever since that specific day, it’s like a switch just flipped off inside me. \n\nOne year and seven months of just... nothing. I keep looking at the calendar, watching the weeks and the months just tick by, and I keep thinking to myself, maybe this month will be the one where I finally wake up and feel like myself again. Maybe I'll finally enjoy that Sunday breakfast with my wife, or feel good about a project finishing up. But... no. Nineteen months of just absolute flatness since that retrofit went wrong. It’s crazy how you can pinpoint the exact moment your life just stalls out, you know? You'd think time changes things, but it’s been a year and seven months and I’m still just stuck in this exact same empty place, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, my appetite... I don't know, it's maybe a little off, but nothing major. But sleep? Sleep is... sleep is just a nightmare, honestly. I mean, literally and figuratively. It’s been this way for the whole nineteen months. \n\nI’ll get into bed, right? My wife will fall asleep almost instantly, like she always does. But me, I just lie there. I lie there and stare at the ceiling, and my brain just starts... it just starts replaying the plant. The layout, the machinery, the retrofit plans, the exact way everything looked that day. I just see it all over and over again in my head, like a movie I can't turn off. I try to think about other things, I try reading, I try listening to those white noise apps, but nothing stops it. My mind is just stuck on the plant. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally manage to drift off, which takes hours, it’s not even real sleep. I'll have these dreams... these awful dreams about the assembly line, about things going wrong, about the accident. And I’ll just jolt awake. My heart will be pounding, I'll be sweating, and I'm just wide awake again. Or, even if I don't have the dreams, I'll just wake up at 3:00 or 4:00 in the morning for absolutely no reason. My eyes just pop open, and that's it. The night is over. I just lie there in the dark until the alarm goes off, and when I finally get up, I feel like I haven't rested at all. I feel like I just ran a marathon instead of sleeping. It’s exhausting, doctor. It’s just so incredibly exhausting to never, ever get a good night's rest anymore. Every single night is just this battle, and I'm losing it every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about something related to the retrofit accident at work?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Ashamed? I... yeah. Yeah, I do. All the time. It's... it's just this constant, heavy feeling, you know? Because I was the manager. I was the one who drew up the schedules, who checked the safety protocols, who signed off on the machinery placements for that entire retrofit. And now... now I just sit there at my desk, and I look at a spreadsheet or a maintenance log, and I freeze. I literally just freeze up. \n\nI keep thinking I can't trust my own judgment anymore. Not even a little bit. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I miss one single detail—just one tiny detail on a schedule, or if I don't double-check a machine setting for the fifth time, somebody else is going to get hurt. And it's going to be my fault. Again. I mean, I've been doing this job for almost twenty years. I have a degree in this stuff. I used to be able to glance at a schematic and know exactly what needed to be done, but now? Now I second-guess every single decision I make, no matter how small. \n\nI'll stare at an email approving a standard parts order for an hour before I send it, just terrified that somehow, in some way I can't see, I've messed it up and it's going to cause another disaster. I just... I feel like I'm a danger to the people who work for me, doctor. Like I'm just waiting to make another mistake that's going to ruin someone else's life. How are you supposed to do your job when you feel like you can't even trust your own mind to keep people safe? It's just this overwhelming guilt that I missed something back then, and this absolute terror that I'm going to miss something again.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends or have you lost interest in social activities as well?\n\nPatient: Friends? Social activities? I... I don't really do any of that anymore. I mean, I used to. We used to have people over for barbecues, or I'd go grab a beer with the guys from the plant after a long shift, especially when we were deep into the planning phases of the retrofit. But now, it's just... I can't. I just can't bring myself to do it. The thought of sitting around and making small talk about sports or the weather or whatever else people talk about when they haven't had their entire world turned upside down... it just feels impossible. It feels pointless, honestly. \n\nAnd it's not even just with friends, doctor. It's... it's right in my own house. It's with my own family. My wife, my kids... I love them, I really do, but... it's like there's this invisible barrier between us now. Like, we'll sit down for dinner, right? A regular family meal, like we've done thousands of times before. My wife will be talking about her day at work, and my son will be going on and on about some video game or something that happened at school, and they're laughing and joking around. And I'm just sitting there at the head of the table, and I'm looking at them, and I'm hearing the words they're saying, but... it's like I'm sitting behind a pane of thick glass. \n\nI can see them, I can hear the muffled sounds of them laughing, but I just... I don't feel connected to it at all. I feel like an alien sitting in their dining room. I feel like I don't really belong in that room with them anymore. Like they're living in this normal, happy world, and I'm just trapped in this cold, empty bubble, watching them from the outside. I try to nod and smile and pretend I'm engaged, but inside, I'm just completely detached from the whole thing. I don't feel the warmth, I don't feel the joy... I just feel this massive, echoing distance between me and the people I'm supposed to be closest to. It's like the accident didn't just break my ability to do my job, it broke my ability to just... be a person around other people. So, no, I don't go out with friends anymore. I can barely even sit through a dinner with my own family without feeling like a complete ghost.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself since the accident?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? No. No, doctor, I... I haven't had any thoughts like that. It's... it's actually almost the exact opposite, if that makes any sense. \n\nI mean, with everything that happened at the plant, the accident, the way everything just went so horribly wrong so fast... I'm terrified of getting hurt. I'm terrified of *anyone* getting hurt. The idea of intentionally doing something to myself? It just... it doesn't even compute in my brain right now. My brain is so hyper-focused on keeping everyone safe, on checking and double-checking every single little thing so disaster doesn't strike again, that the thought of causing harm on purpose is just... no. \n\nPlus, even with how disconnected I feel from my wife and my kids—like I was just saying, sitting at that dining room table feeling like a total ghost—I still know I have a responsibility to them. I'm still their father, you know? I'm still the provider. I still need to be here. If I did something like that... if I hurt myself, after all the trauma we've already been through with the retrofit disaster and the fallout from that... it would just completely destroy them. And I already feel guilty enough about what happened at work. I couldn't possibly add that to the pile. \n\nSo, no, even on the absolute darkest nights when I'm staring at the ceiling at 3:00 in the morning and my mind is just racing and replaying the machinery and the schedules over and over... I mean, I just want the noise to stop, I want the memories to stop, but I don't want to... I don't want to end it all, you know? I just want to figure out how to fix whatever is broken in my head so I can go back to being the guy I was before all this happened. I just want to be normal again, not... not hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks to the accident at the plant?\n\nPatient: Flashbacks... I mean, I don't know if they're exactly like what you see in the movies, where the whole room disappears, but... yes. God, yes. It happens constantly. And it's never when I expect it, either. It's the most mundane things that just... they just rip me right out of whatever I'm doing and drag me back to that day. \n\nFor example, just the other week, they were testing the standard plant alarms. Just a routine drill, right? We do them all the time, it's protocol. But the absolute second that siren went off, that high-pitched wail, my chest just completely seized up. Or even just sitting at my desk in my office—if I have to pull up a retrofit checklist, just a standard blank form that I've used a thousand times in my career to verify equipment specs... I look at those little empty boxes and my hands just start shaking. \n\nAnd the smells... doctor, the smells are honestly the worst part. If the HVAC kicks in a certain way and brings in a draft from the fabrication floor, and I catch even just a faint whiff of hot metal? It's over. I'm completely gone. It throws me right back into that exact second. It's not just a memory, it's like I'm physically standing there again. I can feel the heat in the air, and then... then I just see him. I see him lying there on the concrete floor. I can see the exact angle he was at, the way the machinery was just... \n\nAnd the noise. The yelling. I can hear every single person screaming for help, shouting to cut the main power, radioing frantically for the medics. It's so loud in my head, doctor. It's not just remembering that people yelled, it's *hearing* their voices all over again, cracking with panic. Every time I smell that hot metal or hear a buzzer, it's like my brain doesn't know that it's months later and I'm safe in my office. My brain thinks the accident is happening right then and there all over again. I'll just be gripping the edge of my desk, sweating through my shirt, hearing those screams echoing in my ears until someone knocks on my door and snaps me out of it. \n\nAnd then I have to sit there and pretend I'm fine? Pretend I didn't just watch a man's life get ruined right in front of my eyes for the hundredth time that week? It's exhausting. It's just completely exhausting, because I can't escape it. I can't turn off the plant alarms, I can't stop using checklists to do my job, and I work in a manufacturing plant, for God's sake, there's always going to be the smell of hot metal somewhere. So I'm just constantly a sitting duck, just sitting there waiting for the next normal, everyday thing to send me right back to seeing him on that floor.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities that remind you of the accident at the plant?\n\nPatient: Avoiding... yeah. Yeah, I do. I mean, as a manager, part of my job is being out there, right? I've always prided myself on being a hands-on guy, the kind of manager who doesn't just sit behind a desk looking at spreadsheets but actually gets out on the floor, talks to the operators, checks the equipment. But since it happened... I can't. I completely avoid that specific production line. I avoid it whenever I possibly can. \n\nIf I have to walk from the administrative wing over to the loading docks, I will literally map out a completely different route in my head before I even leave my office. I'll take the long way around, walk all the way through packaging, go down the back stairwell, whatever it takes, even if it adds ten or fifteen minutes to my walk, just so I don't have to pass by that section of the floor. And if there's an issue on that line that needs a manager's eyes on it? I just... I find an excuse. I'll call up one of the floor supervisors, or I'll grab my assistant manager, and I'll tell them I'm bogged down on a conference call or drowning in paperwork, and I'll just send someone else to walk it. I make them do it. I feel incredibly guilty about it, doctor, I really do, because I'm pushing my own responsibilities onto my team, but my feet literally will not move in that direction. The thought of standing in that specific spot again... I just can't do it.\n\nAnd it's not even just the physical places, it's... it's the talking, too. You know how it is in a manufacturing plant, people talk. It's a tight-knit group, and everyone has a theory or a rumor they heard. But the absolute second people start talking about the rollout, or if they bring up the ongoing investigation... I just shut it down. Immediately. If I walk into the breakroom to get a coffee and I hear two guys discussing what the safety inspectors are looking at, or what went wrong with the retrofit schedule, I will just cut them off right there. I interrupt them, I change the subject, or I just use my authority to tell them flat out that we are not discussing the investigation on company time. \n\nI know they probably talk about me behind my back. They probably think I'm being a hardass manager, or worse, they might think I'm trying to cover my own tracks by not letting anyone talk about it. But that's not it at all. It's just that if I let them keep talking, if I have to stand there and listen to the words 'investigation' or 'rollout' and hear them dissecting every little thing that happened that day... my chest gets so tight I feel like I'm going to pass out. I can't let my mind go back there. So I just shut the conversations down before they can even start. I have to control it, you know? Because if I don't avoid that line, and if I don't stop people from talking about it, I'm just going to fall completely apart right there in the middle of the plant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly alert or on the lookout for potential dangers?\n\nPatient: Overly alert... yeah. Yeah, you could definitely say that. It's like my brain is stuck in overdrive, just scanning, scanning, scanning, every single second of the day. I can't turn it off. \n\nEven though I try to avoid that one specific production line like I was just telling you, I still have to be out on the floor for other things, and when I am... it's exhausting. I am constantly, obsessively checking everything. I'll be walking down the aisle and I'm staring at the pressure gauges on the boilers, reading the dials, making sure they're in the green, even though we have dedicated operators for that. I'm constantly checking where the nearest exits are, calculating in my head exactly how many seconds it would take to get a crew out of there if something blew. \n\nAnd the guys working the machines... I find myself just staring at their hands. Just watching their hands like a hawk, making sure they aren't getting too close to the pinch points, making sure they aren't rushing and skipping a safety step. I'm constantly mapping out exactly where every single emergency stop button is. Like, if I'm standing near assembly station four, my brain is repeating, 'The E-stop is exactly three paces to your left, just reach out your left hand.' I rehearse it over and over. I have to know exactly how to shut everything down in a split second.\n\nAnd it doesn't even stop when I get off the floor. That's the crazy part. Even when I'm in the administrative wing, where it's quiet and perfectly safe... like, when we have our weekly management meetings in the main conference room. I used to just stroll in right at 9:00 AM, coffee in hand, and grab whatever chair was empty. I can't do that anymore. I literally have to show up five or ten minutes early now just so I can pick a very specific seat. I have to sit at the far end of the table where I can see the door. I have to have a clear line of sight to the hallway. \n\nIf I get there late and the only open chair is the one with its back to the door? Forget it. I won't hear a single word of the quarterly reports or the budget projections. I have to see everything coming. I have to be ready. It's like I'm just waiting for the other shoe to drop, constantly, and if I let my guard down for even one second, someone else is going to get hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities since the accident?\n\nPatient: Focusing? I mean... no, I wouldn't say I have trouble focusing on my actual tasks. But honestly, when I sit down at my desk, I can still grind through a fifty-page quarterly projection report without missing a beat. \n\nI mean, sure, like I was telling you earlier, if a siren goes off or if I smell that hot metal, then yeah, I'm completely derailed. No... no, I can't say I've ever experienced anything like that. Honestly, doctor, it's almost the exact opposite for me. I feel like I'm way too trapped inside my body, if that makes sense. \n\nI mean, I've heard of people having that kind of thing, you know, where they say they feel like they're floating above themselves or like they're watching a movie of their own life playing out. I felt this massive, overwhelming surge of frustration and anger just bubble up out of nowhere. Their minds just sort of go blank to protect them. And honestly, I kept waiting for that to happen to me. Like, a massive jolt goes right through me, like I literally think something is exploding all over again. My shoulders fly up to my ears, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears, and I just freeze. And the worst part is, it doesn't just pass in a second. You'd think, okay, you realize it's just a door, you calm down. But I don't. The adrenaline just floods my system, and it takes me several minutes—sometimes five or ten minutes—just sitting there, gripping the edge of my desk or leaning against a wall, before my hands actually stop shaking enough for me to even hold a pen or type on my keyboard. \n\nAnd it's so embarrassing, too. Just a plastic clipboard. It made this sharp *smack* sound, and I practically jumped out of my skin. I spilled my coffee all over my shirt, and I was just standing there, panting like I'd run a marathon, while he's looking at me like I'm completely losing my mind. I had to go lock myself in my office and just put my head between my knees until the shaking stopped. So, yeah, small noises, surprises... they absolutely wreck me now. Every single pressure gauge I pass, my eyes are just glued to it, making sure the needle is right where it's supposed to be. And the exits... I'm always looking at the exits. I'm mentally calculating exactly how many steps it would take to get to the nearest door from wherever I'm standing. I'm even watching people's hands. I'm watching what my guys are doing with their hands, what tools they're holding, watching to see if they're about to make a mistake or drop something or turn a valve the wrong way. I just can't have my back exposed like that. I have to be able to see who or what is coming through that door at all times. \n\nSo, yeah, I am always on the lookout. Always. No... no, definitely not. Honestly, just like with the physical stuff I was just telling you about, it’s the exact opposite. I feel like the volume dial on every single emotion I have is just cranked all the way up to maximum, twenty-four hours a day. \n\nI don't feel numb at all. If anything, I feel everything way too intensely now. Like, the fear and the stress... it’s not some distant, foggy thing. It is right there, sitting heavy right on my chest all the time. And it’s not just the fear, it’s everything else, too. Like just the other night, my wife asked me a totally normal, simple question about what we were doing for dinner, and I just snapped. I felt this massive, overwhelming surge of frustration and anger just bubble up out of nowhere. I didn't want to be mad, but the emotion was just so raw and so close to the surface. \n\nI remember hearing once that when people go through terrible things, sometimes they just shut down. Their minds just sort of go blank to protect them. And honestly, I kept waiting for that to happen to me. I kept thinking that any day now, I would just go numb and I wouldn't have to feel this constant, heavy dread anymore. I figured my brain would eventually just decide it had had enough and pull the plug, just to give me a little emotional vacation from the stress of it all. \n\nBut it never happened. Every single morning I wake up and that dread is right there, fresh as ever. I feel the stress, I feel the edge, I feel every single bit of it. It’s exhausting, carrying all that emotion around like a hundred-pound backpack. Sometimes I look at the other guys at the plant, the ones who weren't on shift that day, and they’re just joking around, drinking their coffee, totally relaxed, and I can't even fathom feeling that light anymore. I am just so weighed down by feeling absolutely everything. So, no, I definitely don't feel disconnected from my emotions. I am way, way too plugged in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself startled by small noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Startled? Oh, God, yes. Yes, all the time. It's... it's honestly one of the worst parts of my day now. It's like my nervous system is just completely fried, just constantly set to a hair-trigger. \n\nI mean, working in a manufacturing plant, it's not a quiet environment, you know? There's always something going on. But before the accident, it was just background noise. Now? If a forklift backs up and that little reverse beeper goes off, or if someone accidentally bangs a heavy metal door shut down the hall, or even if one of the routine plant alarms just chirps for a second—which happens all the time for completely harmless reasons, like a pressure valve resetting—my whole body just reacts before my brain even knows what's happening. \n\nI physically jump. Like, a massive jolt goes right through me, like I literally think something is exploding all over again. My shoulders fly up to my ears, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears, and I just freeze. And the worst part is, it doesn't just pass in a second. You'd think, okay, you realize it's just a door, you calm down. But I don't. The adrenaline just floods my system, and it takes me several minutes—sometimes five or ten minutes—just sitting there, gripping the edge of my desk or leaning against a wall, before my hands actually stop shaking enough for me to even hold a pen or type on my keyboard. \n\nAnd it's so embarrassing, too. I'm the manager, right? I'm supposed to be the steady one. But the other day, my assistant manager—the one I mentioned earlier—he just dropped a clipboard on the concrete floor near the breakroom. Just a plastic clipboard. It made this sharp *smack* sound, and I practically jumped out of my skin. I spilled my coffee all over my shirt, and I was just standing there, panting like I'd run a marathon, while he's looking at me like I'm completely losing my mind. It's exhausting. Before... well, before, I used to just walk the floor, you know? I'd talk to the guys, check the production numbers, just do standard manager stuff. But now? Now it's like I'm some kind of safety inspector on steroids. \n\nWhen I'm out on the floor, I am constantly—and I mean *constantly*—checking the gauges. Every single pressure gauge I pass, my eyes are just glued to it, making sure the needle is right where it's supposed to be. And the exits... I'm always looking at the exits. I'm mentally calculating exactly how many steps it would take to get to the nearest door from wherever I'm standing. I'm even watching people's hands. I know that probably sounds a little crazy, but I can't help it. I'm watching what my guys are doing with their hands, what tools they're holding, watching to see if they're about to make a mistake or drop something or turn a valve the wrong way. I make sure I know exactly where the emergency stops are on every single piece of equipment I walk past. I'll just be walking down the aisle, and my brain is just ticking them off: *E-stop is there, E-stop is there, E-stop is there.* \n\nAnd it's not even just when I'm out in the noisy parts of the plant. Even when we're just in the office area, doing administrative stuff... like, if we have a production meeting in the conference room? I literally cannot focus on the meeting if I don't get the right chair. I have to pick a seat where I can see the door. I just can't have my back exposed like that. I have to be able to see who or what is coming through that door at all times. \n\nSo, yeah, I am always on the lookout. Always. I can never just relax and exist in a room anymore. I have to know all the variables. I have to know the escape routes. I just... I can't ever turn it off, doctor. It's like I'm just waiting for the next shoe to drop, twenty-four hours a day.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself constantly looking over your shoulder or checking your surroundings when you're out in public?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, yeah, I mean... I know I just talked your ear off about exactly this at the plant, but it really is the exact same thing everywhere else. It’s the same feeling whether I'm at the grocery store, walking down the street, or at a restaurant. But honestly, my mind always just goes right back to work because that's where the feeling is just so overwhelming. 

Like I was just saying a minute ago, when I'm out on the floor, I am constantly checking the gauges, making sure every single pressure reading is exactly where it should be. I'm checking the exits, I'm watching people's hands... I know I already mentioned keeping track of where the emergency stops are, but it's just this absolute compulsion now, and it completely bleeds into my public life, too. 

If my wife and I go out to dinner, I'm doing exactly what I do in those administrative meetings I told you about. Remember how I said I absolutely have to pick a seat where I can see the door? I do that everywhere now. Every coffee shop, every waiting room, every restaurant. I literally cannot have my back to the entrance or to the rest of the room. I will make the hostess move us if she tries to put me in a booth where my back is exposed. I just sit there, and instead of watching the gauges or my guys' hands on the manufacturing floor, I'm watching the waiters, the other customers, looking at where the fire exits are, checking to see if anyone is carrying anything weird or walking too fast. 

It's like my brain took that intense, buzzing awareness from work and just pasted it over my entire life. So, yes, out in public, I am constantly looking over my shoulder. I'm checking my surroundings non-stop. I can't just go to the hardware store and buy a box of nails anymore. I have to map the place out. I have to know who is in the aisle with me. I just can't turn that radar off, no matter where I am.
